L0282026-10-09work

Reflections as FullProf lists them, checked against 14337 of FullProf's reflections and in all 530 settings

Phase 1, item 4. The module reflections gives, for a phase between two d-spacings (or a 2\(\theta\) range for a wavelength), its unique reflections with multiplicity, d and 2\(\theta\), done the way FullProf does it in CrysFML's CFML_Reflections_Utilities.f90 (read at commit 8ed8670; LGPL-3):

  • Absences come from the operators themselves, as in Hkl_Absent: h is absent when an operator {R|t} has h·R = h and h·t not an integer, which covers centring, screw axes and glide planes. Reflections forbidden only by atoms on special positions stay in the list (222 in F d -3 m, for example).
  • One reflection per set of equivalents, as in Hkl_Uni: the member that lies inside the loop's index box and for which Asu_Hkl(h) = h. Both depend on the space-group number and, for monoclinic settings, on the unique axis.
  • Multiplicity as Hkl_Mult: the distinct h·R and −h·R. Lists are sorted by decreasing d, then h, k, l.

The command line is drs hkl <symbol> <cell> --dmin d or --lambda λ --2theta min max. No external dependency was added.

Tests, all passing:

  • fullprof_reflections: the reflection lists of the FullProf .out files (9 phases, 14337 reflections, in P n m a, P 21/c, P 21/a and P 2/m), extracted with file and line by make_fullprof_reflections.py. Row for row the core gives the same h k l (FullProf's own choice of representative), the same multiplicity, d to the six printed decimals and 2\(\theta\) to the three printed, and no reflection more or fewer. 24 pairs come in the other order where FullProf's d values agree to its precision.
  • reflection_properties, over all 530 settings. For an atom on a general position, a structure factor summed directly over the operators vanishes for all 197980 reflections the core calls absent and for none of the 506920 it allows (|h_i| \(\le 5\)). The multiplicities add up to the number of allowed reflections counted one by one in a sphere. FullProf's rule picks exactly one representative per set in every standard setting.
  • Deliberate errors are caught. A wrong absence condition fails 6064 property checks, and a wrong index box gives 6575 disagreements with FullProf.

Found on the way:

  • The cell. FullProf computes the printed list in the last cycle, with the cell before that cycle's shifts, so the reference takes value − shift from the last cell block with a non-zero shift. LBF_lmopr.out gives d(200) = 2.874002 = (5.748007 − 0.000003)/2, and LaMnO3_cub.out gives d(020) = (7.694143 − 0.000014)/2. With the printed final cell every d was off by about 5·\(10^{-7}\) relative.
  • CrysFML's −3m1 region alone admits two members of some sets, e.g. (0 4 1) and (4 0 −1). FullProf's choice also needs the index box of its loop (l \(\ge 0\) there), which the port now applies.
  • Monoclinic settings named "-c2" and the like take their unique axis after the '-', as CrysFML reads it; without that, twelve centred settings overcounted their multiplicities.
  • In 42 non-standard settings, the monoclinic settings with unique axis a and the R groups on rhombohedral axes, FullProf's rule does not single out one member per set. CrysFML's own code calls the a-axis rule "Provisional (to be tested)". There the core uses a stated fallback (the largest h, k, l) and marks those reflections.
  • One pair in PPH3-sls_1.out (Prof_Match): (7 1 2), d = 1.489789, is listed before (1 10 0), d = 1.489798, against FullProf's own printed d and η, and with the starting cell as well. This could not be explained from the material; the test lists it and fails if a second such pair appears.

Not checked: FullProf's choice in Laue classes other than mmm and 2/m, which have no FullProf list in the material. There the port of CrysFML's rules and the property tests stand in.

L0262026-10-09work

Sites: Wyckoff positions and special-position constraints, checked against spglib and FullProf

Phase 1, item 3. The module site takes a position in a space group and gives:

  • the site-symmetry operators (exact, with their lattice translation), the site point group, the multiplicity and the orbit in the cell;
  • the Wyckoff letter and oriented site-symmetry symbol, for any of the 530 settings;
  • the constraints the site puts on x, y, z, solved from (R − I)·x = −t, and on the betas, from R·β·Rᵀ = β.

Constraints are exact linear relations over free parameters, value = c + Σ a·p, with the earliest variables free as FullProf picks them (x, 1/4, z; x, 2x, z; β22 = β11, β12 = β11/2). That is the form the refinement engine will use. The command line is drs site <symbol> <x> <y> <z>. No external dependency was added.

Two tables come from outside, as names only, with their generators kept in core/tests/data:

  • the Wyckoff positions of the 530 settings (letter, multiplicity, site symbol, coordinates), from spglib's database/Wyckoff.csv (GitHub, commit e606dfa);
  • FullProf's space-group symbols with their Hall symbols, from CrysFML's Spgr_Info table (commit 8ed8670 of the copy on GitHub, LGPL-3). With it the core reads the older symbols of .pcr files, such as "B m a b" and "F m 3 m". A group given by any Hall symbol is matched to its table setting by its operators.

Tests, all passing:

  • spglib_wyckoff: every Wyckoff position of the standard setting of all 230 types (1731 positions, at two sets of free parameters: 3462). spglib's get_symmetry_dataset analysed a structure with an atom on the position and a second species on a general position (reference written in the test-data step by make_wyckoff_reference.py). The core finds the same multiplicity, letter and site symbol, also from four other images of each position. The site point group matches spglib's symbol. Multiplicity × site order = group order (the property test). The number of free coordinates is that of the Wyckoff triplet. The number of free betas equals the dimension of the invariant tensors, counted separately by the character formula.
  • u_ij_follow_beta_relations: in all 530 settings no rotation mixes axes of different length, so the U_ij of a CIF obey the same relations as the betas.
  • fullprof_sites: the atoms of the FullProf files, extracted with file and line by make_fullprof_sites.py. The core gives FullProf's multiplicity for all 96 atoms of the 13 .out files. For the 69 atoms refined with anisotropic betas in the .pcr files (P n m a, F m m m, P 21/a, I 21 3, B m a b, F m 3 m), the refined betas satisfy the core's relations to the printed decimals, and betas fixed by symmetry carry code 0. In the 16 cases where a tied beta and its partner both carry codes, the codes encode the same relation (same parameter, multipliers in the ratio).
  • crysfml_symbols: 583 of CrysFML's 612 symbols are table settings, with the right number and the same setting where the two tables share a name, and the lookup finds each from its FullProf name. The other 29 are cells outside the 530 (A 1, I -1, F 2/m, C 4 2 21, F 4/m m m, ":1" origins of groups without origin choices), listed by the test.
  • Deliberate errors are caught. A wrong sign in the beta equations fails all 3462 positions and 550 FullProf checks. A loose tolerance in the Wyckoff search fails 398 positions.

Found on the way:

  • spglib keeps a structure in its input setting only for the standard settings; in the other 300 settings it moves to the standard one, so Wyckoff letters were checked in the 230 standard settings.
  • spglib reduces triclinic cells, so the triclinic test cells are already reduced.
  • CrysFML writes the Hall symbol of P 3 2 1 as "P 3 2""" (Fortran's doubled quote); the first extraction cut it to "P 3 2" (which is P 3 1 2), and crysfml_symbols caught it.

Not checked: letters in non-standard settings against an outside analysis (the table's letters are used as given); the International Tables directly.

L0242026-10-09work

Space groups from Hall symbols: all 530 settings agree with spglib and with FullProf

Phase 1, item 2 (Q2, L022). Three modules in the core:

  • symop: general symmetry operators {R|t}, R an integer matrix and t exact in twelfths; product, kind of rotation, reading and writing "x,y,z" triplets.
  • spacegroup: a parser of Hall symbols (lattice symbol, up to four matrix symbols with Hall's default axes, change of origin). It builds the generators, closes the group, and derives the centring vectors, the inversion (and whether it is at the origin), the point group, the Laue class and the crystal system.
  • sgtable: the 530 settings (Hall number, number, choice, Hall and Hermann–Mauguin symbols), taken from spglib 2.7.0's table of settings by core/tests/data/make_sgtable.py.

Only names come from that table; the operators are generated by the core. The command line is drs sg <symbol>, with a number, a Hermann–Mauguin symbol (":choice" for a setting) or a Hall symbol, and drs sg --list. No external dependency was added. Tests:

  • spglib_530: for every one of the 530 settings, the generated set of operators is spglib's stored set (spglib's operator table, separate from its Hall symbols; 7388 operators compared exactly), and the point group, crystal system, centring and centrosymmetry agree. No disagreement. spglib was installed with pip only to write the reference file core/tests/data/spglib_530.txt.
  • fullprof_spacegroups: all 13 FullProf .out files (P n m a, P 21/c, P 21/a, P 2/m). FullProf's symbol is found in the table with FullProf's Hall symbol, number and setting choice. The crystal system, Laue class, point group, multiplicity and centrosymmetry agree, and FullProf's operators are the core's. No disagreement.
  • A deliberate error in one rotation matrix (the two-fold along y) is caught by both tests. Found on the way:
  • The first run agreed on 525 of 530. The five rhombohedral-axes settings ("P 3* 2", R32 to R-3c) need the two-fold after the three-fold along a+b+c to lie along a−b; the parser now does so.
  • spglib names every setting of a monoclinic type by the type's symbol ("P 2_1/c" for all nine settings of no. 14). The short symbol of each setting is therefore its full symbol with the 1s dropped, the International Tables' monoclinic rule, so that FullProf's "P 21/a" finds -P 2yab.
  • One unit-test value written from memory (18 settings of no. 14) was wrong; spglib's table has 9. Not checked: the International Tables, Vol. A, directly (no copy in the repository); FullProf covers only four groups.

L0232026-10-09work

Phase 1 opens: the Rust core, and the unit cell checked against all thirteen FullProf output files

The crate drs in core/: a library for the app and the drs command line, no external dependencies. Its first module is the unit cell: direct and reciprocal parameters, the metric tensors G and G*, the volume, d(hkl) and 2\(\theta\), FullProf's Cartesian frame (x along a, y in the ab-plane) and the Busing–Levy B matrix. Conventions are in core/README.md.

The named test fullprof_metric reads the "Metric information" block of every .out file in manuals/fullprof/ (13 files: LaMnO3 orthorhombic, and citric acid and PPH3 in P2₁/c, monoclinic), takes the cell FullProf printed, recomputes the 64 other numbers of the block and compares each with FullProf's: no disagreement. The tolerance is half a unit of the last printed digit plus 5·\(10^{-5}\) of the quantity's largest element, which covers starting from the rounded printed cell; on PPH3, the volume from the printed cell is 1456.935 Å³ against FullProf's 1456.927 Å³. Seven unit tests add the cubic, hexagonal and triclinic cases, 2\(\theta\) at exactly 90° and 180°, and the refusal of impossible cells.

Found on the way: a test value typed from memory (Si 111 at 28.443°, which is the value for the mean Cu Kα, not Kα1) was replaced by exact geometric cases, and the core now accepts back-scattering at 2\(\theta\) = 180°, which rounding had put just beyond the limiting sphere.

L0222026-10-09decision

Q2, Q4, Q5, Q6 decided; phase 0 closes

Josu takes the recommendations made in the conversation for the four design questions still open:

  • Q2, space groups: the 230 types in their 530 settings generated from Hall symbols, not typed in, each tested against spglib and the International Tables, Vol. A. Symmetry stored as general operators (rotation and translation), so that magnetic and superspace groups, "later" in L012, can be added without a rewrite.
  • Q4, the editor: CodeMirror 6 inside the Tauri window, with a language mode for our control file (L020).
  • Q5, the live plot: every least-squares cycle is an event from the core (parameters and shifts, calculated curve, agreement factors); the app redraws on each event, and the events form the refinement history.
  • Q6, the Mac app: document-based, as the difes shell: a project per window or tab, the full menu bar, Settings, Recent items, Undo and Redo of every model change, source list, inspector, ⌘K palette.

With Q1 (L021) and Q3 (L020), every design question has a logged decision, the FullProf inventory and the GSAS-II gap list are on the site, and the proposal's questions are answered: the "done when" of phase 0 is met and phase 0 closes. Phase 1, the crystallography core, is written out on the roadmap. Set aside: typed-in space-group tables (error-prone, and nothing to test them against but themselves); a native editor component (CodeMirror 6 already has completion, folding and diagnostics); drawing only at the end of a refinement.

conversation of 9 October 2026 · Design · Roadmap
L0212026-10-09decision

Q1: the core in Rust, with Python as its scripting interface

Josu decides design question Q1. The core is written in Rust; Python is the interface for scripting, through bindings to the Rust core (PyO3), in version 2 as the proposal has it (P48). No physics is written in Python. The reasons, from the comparison made in the conversation:

  • Tauri's side is Rust, so the core links straight into the Mac app: one signed binary, no interpreter or local server to ship and start (the difes-shell way, which works but is heavier).
  • The same crate builds the command-line program that every feature needs (CLAUDE.md), and its tests run with cargo test in a cloud session as well as on the Mac.
  • Most of the code is written by Claude sessions; Rust's compiler rejects whole classes of errors (types, memory, data races) before any test runs.
  • Speed is that of Fortran or C++ for the inner loop (profile, structure factors, derivatives).
  • Scripting, the strength of GSAS-II (L014), is kept: Python users drive the Rust core through the bindings.

The cost accepted: few crystallographic libraries in Rust. The crystallography is written in phase 1 and checked against independent sources (the FullProf test files in manuals/fullprof/, spglib, the International Tables). Set aside: a Python core with compiled kernels (the GSAS-II and difes route: the friendliest for Josu to read and change, but an interpreter inside the app); a Fortran core (FullProf's language and CrysFML, but awkward to link into Tauri and weak tooling); C++ (no advantage over Rust here, memory errors possible, complex builds).

conversation of 9 October 2026 · Design, Q1 · Proposal, P48
L0202026-10-09decision

The proposal's open questions decided: constant wavelength first, Levenberg–Marquardt, our own control file

Josu answers the six questions the proposal draft (L019) left open, taking the recommendations made in the conversation:

  • TOF (P05): version 2. Version 1 is constant wavelength only (laboratory X-rays, synchrotron, reactor neutrons).
  • Joint X-ray + neutron refinement (P06): version 2, since it needs the multi-pattern machinery of version 2; the version 1 data model allows several patterns from the start.
  • Rigid bodies and TLS (P41): version 2. Distance, angle and linear restraints stay in version 1.
  • The minimiser (P42): Levenberg–Marquardt with SVD against correlations, as GSAS-II. With correlated parameters it is more robust than FullProf's Gauss–Newton with relaxation factors, which need hand tuning.
  • The control file (P47, design question Q3): a new readable text format of our own, written and read by the core, with an importer for FullProf .pcr files so that FullProf users bring their work.
  • The reference refinements that define "version 1 is done": the four tutorials proposed, Y2O3, MgAl2O4, Co-doped YBCO and quantitative phase analysis, each to be reproduced within its esd's from the command line and the app.

Set aside: TOF, joint refinement and rigid bodies in version 1, which keeps version 1 to what a FullProf user does today on constant-wavelength data; Gauss–Newton with relaxation factors; the .pcr itself as the app's file format (fixed-format, numeric codewords, not readable without the manual). Still open: Q1, the language of the core, and with it P48 (Python scripting in version 1 or 2).

conversation of 9 October 2026 · Proposal · data/proposal.yml
L0192026-10-09work

The proposal drafted: 59 features, 38 in version 1

The last item of phase 0, written as a draft for Josu to decide, not as a decision. The app's feature set is drawn from the FullProf inventory (199 entries, L017) and the GSAS-II gap list (36 gaps, L018). There are 59 features in 11 groups; each names its sources (inventory entries, gaps, brief requirements), and build.py stops if a named entry does not exist. Draft tiers: 38 in version 1, 11 in version 2, 10 later; the L012 "later" list stays outside both versions.

  • Version 1: what a FullProf user does today on constant-wavelength data (laboratory X-rays, synchrotron, reactor neutrons), from pattern to CIF, one or several phases, quantitative analysis, Le Bail, constraints, limits, distance/angle/linear restraints. It runs inside the app the brief asks for: one project document with undo, a readable control file with .pcr import, builder, editor, live plot per cycle, background set on the plot.
  • Where GSAS-II does it better, the draft takes the GSAS-II way: named parameters instead of numeric codewords, constraint equations, the s.u. of any expression, accept/reject and a refinement history, vendor formats read directly, publication plots.
  • Version 2: TOF, joint multi-pattern refinement, sequential and parametric refinement, rigid bodies and TLS, anisotropic size and strain, texture beyond one axis, Pawley, refinement diagnostics, Python scripting.
  • Proposed test for "version 1 is done": the command line and the app reproduce four reference refinements from manuals/fullprof/ (Y2O3, MgAl2O4, Co-doped YBCO, the quantitative-analysis tutorial) within their esd's.

Open for Josu: TOF, joint X-ray + neutron and rigid bodies in version 1 or 2; the minimiser (Levenberg–Marquardt with SVD, or Gauss–Newton with relaxation); the control-file format (Q3); the reference refinements.

Proposal (draft) · data/proposal.yml
L0182026-10-09finding

GSAS-II gaps re-checked against FullProfAPP: none closed, five narrowed

The 36 gaps of L014 were re-checked against the FullProfAPP manual (version 1.3.3, June 2025; first release 1.0.0, July 2023; Windows and Linux only), which L014 could not reach. Each gap was also checked against the 41 capabilities added to the FullProf inventory in L017. The manual closes none and narrows five; each narrowed row now says what FullProfAPP does and what remains of the gap:

  • Automatic background: FullProfAPP finds points by asymmetric least squares and fits a Chebyshev series to them. What remains: other functions, and the automatic background used as a fixed background.
  • Project file: a .fpapp file saves the interface state and each run goes to a numbered folder. What remains: data, model, constraints, results and covariance in one file.
  • Python scripting: documented modules edit .pcr files and parse .sum, .prf and .mic files. What remains: running refinements from a script, the covariance, constraint equations.
  • Publication plots: plots can be saved from a context menu. What remains: named vector and program formats.
  • Sequential results: exported as XY text. What remains: a multi-block CIF of the series.

None of the L017 additions closes a gap. For example, spherical harmonics in FullProf are for anisotropic strain, not for preferred orientation, and the stored background profiles are not GSAS-II's background peaks. The gap list stays at 36.

L0172026-10-09finding

The FullProf inventory brought up to date: 199 capabilities

The L013 inventory was written from the July 2001 manual and the tutorials and tests. It has now been checked against the material added in L016: the FullProf News pages 2001–2026, the TOF draft (December 2003), the microstructure text, GLOpSAnn, the MolPDF guide and the FullProfAPP manual (version 1.3.3, June 2025). Four readings proposed 53 additions and about 120 amendments. After merging duplicates, 41 capabilities were added and 80 existing entries amended. Each addition cites the News page or document and carries the date and version it appeared ("Since …", 62 entries). There are now 199 entries: data 18, structure 16, profile 28, background 12, size/strain 14, corrections 18, phases 6, Le Bail 6, constraints 15, engine 14, workflow 34, output 18.

Added within the scope, among others:

  • Profile: TOF lookup tables for α, β, \(\sigma\)², \(\gamma\) in the resolution file (December 2005, extended 2009 and 2011), TCH pseudo-Voigt for TOF data (January 2021, version 7.40), TOF_fit_LM to build resolution files (January 2021).
  • Background: a refined sum of up to 10 stored profiles (July 2003); automatic background points (FullProfAPP).
  • Microstructure: anisotropic strain by spherical harmonics (May 2006).
  • Corrections: multi-axial March–Dollase and the anvil-cell model (Nor=2, 3; October 2003), Lobanov–Alte da Veiga cylinder absorption and flat-plate transmission with a PSD (April 2002), a per-reflection correction file (2004).
  • Workflow: sequential multi-pattern refinement (April 2014), CIFs_to_PCR (May 2016), WinPLOTR-2006, and nine FullProfAPP capabilities (2023–2025: batch and multi-pattern grid, full-profile phase search against the COD, protocols saved as JSON, a parallel job scheduler).

Corrected: Res=5 has always been the keyword TOF resolution file (since January 2003), and the interpolated tables were added inside it later. VARY/FIX, DLIM and COMMANDS date from 2002–2005. The Howard asymmetry was removed in January 2001. Ikeda–Carpenter left the not-found list (the Npr=13 entry now has the formula). The texture item was reworded: no spherical-harmonic model, but multi-axial March–Dollase exists.

L0142026-10-09finding

GSAS-II against the FullProf baseline: 36 gaps

GSAS-II, read from its source and tutorials repositories (L011), against the FullProf inventory (L013). Only what GSAS-II has and FullProf lacks within the L012 scope is kept. Each gap was checked in the GSAS-II file and line it cites, and the FullProf material (manual, FP_COMMANDS, tutorials, tests) was searched for it. Each row says what FullProf has instead. Per area: workflow 7, constraints 5, output 5, data 4, engine 4, background 3, corrections 3, profile 2, structure 1, microstructure 1, Le Bail 1. The gaps that matter for the design:

  • Workflow: one project file with a data tree instead of .pcr plus side files; a Python scripting API; accepting or rejecting a refinement; a project notebook; pseudo-variables and parametric fits of sequential results; limits and excluded regions set on the plot.
  • Constraints: equations and new-variable parameters, as against FullProf's shared codewords; histogram wildcards in sequential fits; general restraints as Python expressions; plane and chiral restraints; Mogul import.
  • Engine: Levenberg–Marquardt with SVD instead of damped Gauss–Newton; "parameter impact"; the s.u. of any expression from the full covariance; a normal probability plot.
  • Physics: Pawley (FullProf does Le Bail only); pink-beam profiles; instrument files derived from fundamental parameters; background peaks and automatic background; spherical-harmonic preferred orientation and texture; extinction for CW powder data; Gauss–Lorentz mixing of anisotropic size.

Rejected because FullProf has them too: per-histogram cells, rigid bodies with TLS, parameter limits, named parameters (VARY/EQUAL), Durbin–Watson, the live plot each cycle, waterfall plots, CIF export, symmetry-mode refinement, Fourier maps, parallel runs (fp2k_par). Limits: neither program was run, so GUI behaviour comes from help pages and code. The FullProf side is what manuals/fullprof/ documents; the FullProfAPP manual (fullprofapp.readthedocs.io, blocked here) may close some workflow gaps.

GSAS-II · data/study/gsas2.yml · GSAS-II@c13e730, GSAS-II-tutorials@924e9eb · manuals/fullprof/
L0132026-10-09finding

The FullProf inventory: 158 capabilities for powder structure refinement

FullProf's capabilities within the L012 scope, read from manuals/fullprof/: the July 2001 manual page by page, FP_COMMANDS, tutorials 1–11 and FP_tests (308 files, 130 .pcr). There are 158 entries, each saying what the capability does, where it is set in the .pcr, and its sources. All 435 citations were checked against the page or line they name, and 15 were corrected. Per area: data 16, structure 16, profile 23, background 9, size/strain 13, corrections 12, phases 5, Le Bail 6, constraints 14, engine 12, workflow 17, output 15. Six capabilities are kept for later, one line each: magnetic, superspace, stacking faults (FAULTS), indexing, structure solution, PDF. Out of scope: single crystal, flipping ratios, electron diffraction. Findings:

  • The manual describes FullProf.2k. Twenty-five capabilities are documented only by the tutorials and tests, not in the 2001 manual: symmetry-mode refinement (Jbt=6), Chebyshev and Fourier backgrounds (Nba=-5, -4), COMMANDS keywords, sequential runs, FullProfAPP, the Bérar factor printed in .out files.
  • Not found in the material: Pawley fitting (FullProf does Le Bail), surface-roughness corrections by name, thin-sample transparency (the manual says it "is not implemented"), texture beyond the exponential and March–Dollase functions, Sabine extinction for CW powders, fundamental-parameters peak shapes, hkl-dependent asymmetry, an amorphous phase as a phase, the Ikeda–Carpenter parameters (Npr=13 named only).
  • These "not found" items are what GSAS-II and Jana2020 are read against first.
FullProf · data/study/fullprof.yml · manuals/fullprof/
L0122026-10-09decision

FullProf as the baseline; GSAS-II and Jana2020 read only for what FullProf lacks

Josu revises the method of L009. The three programs are no longer read side by side on one template. Instead:

  • FullProf is the baseline. Its complete feature inventory for crystal-structure refinement from powder data is built from manuals/fullprof/: the manual, FP_COMMANDS, tutorials 1–11 and FP_tests. Each capability gets one entry saying what it does, with its .pcr reference and its source (path, page), not a copy of the .pcr format.
  • GSAS-II and Jana2020 are read only for what FullProf lacks within that scope, in physics and in workflow: project handling, sequential and parametric refinement, constraints and restraints, scripting, GUI. Each gap cites its source.
  • In scope: Rietveld refinement of crystal structures (atoms, occupancies, isotropic and anisotropic displacements, cell); Le Bail and Pawley; peak shapes and instrument description for laboratory X-rays, synchrotron, CW and TOF neutrons; background; size and strain broadening; preferred orientation, absorption, zero and displacement corrections; several phases per pattern, hence quantitative phase analysis; constraints, restraints, rigid bodies.
  • Later, one line each in the inventory saying which program has it and where: magnetic structures, modulated/superspace, stacking faults, indexing, structure solution, 2D image integration, PDF/total scattering.
  • Out: single crystal, 3D electron diffraction. The reason is that one baseline read in depth, plus differences, gives a feature list the app can be designed from directly, without a three-way matrix of overlapping entries. Set aside: the common-template page per program and the three-way feature matrix (roadmap items replaced). The GSAS-II page of L011 is kept as source material and rewritten as a gap list. The L011 findings on magnetic, superspace, PDF, small-angle and 2D images drop to one line each, or out of scope.
conversation of 9 October 2026 · Roadmap
L0112026-10-09finding

GSAS-II read on the common template

GSAS-II read from its own source and documentation: the GitHub repositories GSAS-II (commit c13e730) and GSAS-II-tutorials (commit 924e9eb), which is the source of the tutorial site Josu pointed to. That site itself is blocked by this session's network, so nothing needs downloading into manuals/. Each claim on the page links to the line it comes from. Findings that matter for the design:

  • There is no control file. Everything lives in one .gpx project shown as a data tree, and the same file is driven from Python (GSASIIscriptable). Every parameter has a name p:h:name:id.
  • The powder plot is updated after every least-squares cycle (a hook called inside the minimiser), and Rw is drawn per cycle: the FullProf-style live display of R14 exists here too.
  • Profiles: Finger–Cox–Jephcoat pseudo-Voigt with Thompson–Cox–Hastings widths (CW); back-to-back exponentials ⊗ pseudo-Voigt (TOF, pink beam); fundamental parameters are used only to make instrument files.
  • Background: eight functions plus Debye diffuse terms, background peaks, a measured background histogram, mouse-set fixed points and an automatic baseline (pybaselines).
  • Minimiser: Levenberg–Marquardt on an analytic Hessian summed per histogram, with SVD against correlations; parameter limits; a "parameter impact" tool; s.u. propagated into any expression.
  • Breadth: magnetic structures through Bilbao k-SUBGROUPSMAG, (3+1)D superspace, spherical-harmonic texture, rigid bodies, PDF, small-angle, reflectometry, 2D images, sequential and parametric fits. Stacking faults are simulated with DIFFaX but cannot yet be fitted.
  • Documentation is 62 tutorials and context help, with no printed manual.
GSAS-II · data/study/gsas2.yml · github.com/AdvancedPhotonSource/GSAS-II@c13e730, GSAS-II-tutorials@924e9eb

Josu added the FullProf documentation to manuals/fullprof/ on main (commits dd656ce, 9fae9ad), outside ecosystem/, so the site Action never publishes it. L0082026-10-08decision

The five programs to study: FullProf, GSAS-II, TOPAS, Jana2020, MAUD

The five most used of the nine on the long list, chosen on measured evidence recorded per program in data/programs.yml and shown as two tables on the Background page. The evidence is citations of each program's reference paper(s) per database, what the neutron and synchrotron facilities name or ship, and what the instrument makers ship. No published survey of program use was found. The rule: rank by the citations of the program's own reference papers, and let facility and vendor evidence decide close cases. A program whose count could not be checked, or cannot be separated from another program's, is not shortlisted on this evidence. FullProf (1993 paper: 20,552 Google Scholar, 11,926 Web of Science; ILL, PSI, ALBA), GSAS-II (GSAS-II 2013: 3,393 and EXPGUI 2001: 7,720 on Semantic Scholar; APS 11-BM, Diamond I11, ALBA) and TOPAS (2018 paper: about 2,263 Google Scholar; the program Diamond I11 mainly supports; Bruker's package) lead clearly. Jana2020 is fourth (Jana2006 paper: 4,071 on Semantic Scholar, about 500 a year by its authors' account), with the caveat that Jana's counts mix single-crystal and powder use. It is kept because it is the only program on the list built around superspace (modulated, composite, magnetic) powder refinement. MAUD is fifth and the closest call (1999 paper: 1,336 Google Scholar, 549 Semantic Scholar; the only program on the list built around combined texture, stress and microstructure analysis). Set aside: RIETAN-FP, whose Google Scholar count (3,149) is higher than MAUD's but belongs to a paper that also introduces VESTA, so its own share cannot be separated; HighScore Plus, shipped by Malvern Panalytical but with no citation count that could be checked (the laboratory-vendor side is in the five through TOPAS); Profex/BGMN (94, Crossref, old snapshot); SmartLab Studio II (PDXL), Rigaku's package, with no peer-reviewed reference paper. Limits: the session's network policy blocked the citation APIs (Crossref, OpenAlex, Semantic Scholar, Europe PMC) and the facility and journal pages, so every number was read from a web-search result quoting the page, with the snapshot date unknown. Databases disagree widely (FullProf: 20,552 Google Scholar, 2,486 Semantic Scholar). ISIS and ESRF pages naming a program were not found. Missing values are marked "not checked". Before the choice is relied on beyond phase 0, it should be re-run from the databases directly, and HighScore Plus and RIETAN-FP are the first to re-examine.

data/programs.yml · Background · Semantic Scholar, Google Scholar author profiles, ILL list of J. Rodríguez-Carvajal's publications, Diamond I11 software FAQ, APS 11-BM user wiki, PSI DMC pages, ALBA MSPD software page, Bruker, Malvern Panalytical and Rigaku product pages (URLs per number in programs.yml)
L0072026-10-08decision

The site published from an empty public repository; the sources stay private

GitHub Pages cannot serve a private repository on the free plan, and the sources (core, app, data) are to stay private. So, as with the difes test ledger, the site is served from a separate public repository, diff-ref-struct-site, which holds nothing but the rendered pages. Unlike the difes twin, it is filled by an Action of the private repository, not by a script on the Mac: every push to main that touches ecosystem/ builds, checks and renders the site with Quarto 1.10.18 and replaces the public repository's content whole. That way a Claude session on the web, which may have no Quarto, still publishes by pushing. The Action writes with a deploy key limited to the public repository (secret SITE_DEPLOY_KEY). Set aside: a paid plan for Pages on the private repository; making the source repository public.

.github/workflows/publish-site.yml · jmigartua.github.io/diff-ref-struct-site

Private repository jmigartua/diff-ref-struct created and pushed. L0052026-10-08decision

Study before code; the core's language not chosen yet

Phase 0 is the study of the programs (R04–R06) and nothing is built before it closes. The language of the core is left open on purpose: Rust (shared with the Tauri side, fast, one binary), Python with compiled kernels (the GSAS-II route and the difes habit) and a Fortran or C++ kernel are the options, and the choice is a design decision taken after the study, with the reasons logged. The long list of programs holds nine candidates; the five to study are chosen with evidence, not from memory.

L0032026-10-08decision

One repository with three folders, not three repositories

The work is to be done in Claude, and a Claude Code session on the web clones one repository. So the project is a single private repository, jmigartua/diff-ref-struct, whose three top folders play the parts of the three difes repositories: core/ (the refinement engine, as difes), app/ (the Tauri Mac app, as difes-shell) and ecosystem/ (data, tools, site and log, as ecosystem_site). The separation of core and app is kept by rule, not by repository: the core never imports the app.

R18, R19
L0022026-10-08finding

How the difes ecosystem is built, the pattern to follow

The difes project lives in three repositories side by side. difes is the physics core in Python: no GUI imports, every feature with a test and a command-line path, a README with conventions, a CHANGELOG generated from the site. difes-shell is a thin Tauri 2 window that starts the core as a local server and draws what it returns; no physics in the shell. ecosystem_site is a Quarto website (theme none, the family design kit) whose navigation, log, release notes and inventory are generated by tools/build.py from data/*.yml on every render, with a plain LOG.md next to it, a manual in two parts (using the program, theoretical basis), a test ledger, an algorithm registry and a download page.

ftir-ecosystem-site, difes, difes-shell (read on 2026-10-08)
L0012026-10-08work

The brief

Josu asks for a macOS app, built with Tauri and behaving as a Mac app, for Rietveld refinement of crystal structures from powder diffraction (laboratory X-rays, synchrotron, neutrons), in the line of FullProf and GSAS. Before any code: an inventory of the five most used refinement programs, what they do and how, the features they share and those unique to each, so that the feature set is the most ambitious one. Asked for by name: the refinement core, a live cycle-by-cycle plot as in FullProf, graphics for the data and for the results, interactive background selection, the complete space-group tables, a GUI that writes the control files, and a text editor made for the control and output files. Everything logged and reported, on a website holding the process, the release notes, the findings, the background and the manuals of the core and the app, worked the way the difes project is. The brief is kept verbatim and organised into nineteen requirements (R01–R19) on the Brief page.

The brief · conversation of 8 October 2026
On this page

    What this is

    The working site of one project: to build a Mac app for Rietveld refinement of powder diffraction data, after a study of the programs the community uses for it. It holds the brief, that study, the design, a dated log of every step, the release notes and the manuals of the core and the app.

    Josu M. Igartua · Departamento de Física, UPV/EHU
    josu.igartua@ehu.eus
